max-md:flex-col w-full min-w-0"><div><h2 id="key-takeaways" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Key Takeaways</h2>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">The Elastic Security Team is tracking an organized and financially-motivated ransomware and extortion group called Cuba Ransomware</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Cuba Ransomware targets small and medium-sized retailers, exfiltrating sensitive information, and then deploying ransomware</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Cuba Ransomware uses a “name and shame” approach by releasing exfiltrated data as an additional method to extort ransomware cryptocurrency payments</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">We are releasing a YARA signature and providing hunting queries that detect this ransomware family</li>
</ul>
<blockquote class="my-7 border-l-4 border-default pl-4 text-lg text-subtler italic">
<p class="mb-6">For information on the CUBA ransomware campaign and associated malware analysis, check out our blog posts detailing this:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/cuba-ransomware-malware-analysis" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">CUBA Malware Analysis</a></li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4"><a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/bughatch-malware-analysis" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">BUGHATCH Malware Analysis</a></li>
</ul>
</blockquote>
<h2 id="preamble" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Preamble</h2>
<p class="mb-6">The Elastic Security Team is tracking a threat group that is leveraging the Cuba Ransomware, combined with data exfiltration and extortion, to target North American and European retailers and manufacturers for cryptocurrency payments. The threat group has followed an effective, but repetitive cluster of TTPs for initial access, lateral movement, exfiltration, ransomware deployment, and extortion.</p>
<h2 id="initial-access" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Initial Access</h2>
<p class="mb-6">The incidents that we have observed included hosts that were infected with a litany of initial access opportunities. These included everything from potentially unwanted programs (PUP) to remotely executable vulnerabilities. Because of this, we cannot verify what the initial access vehicle was, but there are two theories:</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">An access broker</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">A remotely exploitable vulnerability</li>
</ul>
<p class="mb-6">While there are many ways to gain access into a targeted network, we’ll explore the most likely hypotheses for how the CUBA threat group gained access.</p>
<h3 id="access-broker" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Access Broker</h3>
<p class="mb-6">As an introduction, an access broker is a threat group who, as they move through the <a href="https://www.lockheedmartin.com/en-us/capabilities/cyber/cyber-kill-chain.html" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">kill chain</a>, has their “actions on objective” as collecting and maintaining remote access into a targeted network so that access can be sold to other threat groups who have other goals.</p>
<p class="mb-6">This is a common tactic for ransomware campaigns where the goal is to rapidly encrypt and extort victims into paying to recover data. When using ransomware kits (ransomware-as-a-service), the threat actors are often focused on moving rapidly across many victims and not on the reconnaissance required to identify and exploit victims to deploy their ransomware.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Ransomware-as-a-service includes a lot of overhead such as negotiating with victims, troubleshooting unlock procedures, and managing the crypto infrastructure. It is often easier to purchase previously exploited systems that allow the ransomware campaign owners to be “shell wranglers” instead of needing to gain and maintain access to a large number of environments.</p>
<p class="mb-6">The theory that an initial access broker may have been used began percolating because we observed access attempts using an Exchange vulnerability in multiple contested networks; however, all networks did not receive the CUBA ransomware. Additionally, we observed initial access attempts in January but did not observe CUBA ransomware until March which would align with an access broker gaining and maintaining persistence while shopping for a buyer.</p>
<p class="mb-6">In the environments where the CUBA ransomware was not deployed, the incident response was rapid, however incomplete, and access was regained. Once the persistence was observed, the adversary was successfully evicted and CUBA was never deployed.</p>
<h3 id="remotely-exploitable-vulnerability" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Remotely Exploitable Vulnerability</h3>
<p class="mb-6">We observed the execution of the ProxyLogon exploit. <a href="https://www.mandiant.com/resources/unc2596-cuba-ransomware" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>Previous research</u></a> has observed this threat group leveraging <a href="https://cve.mitre.org/cgi-bin/cvename.cgi?name=CVE-2021-26855" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>ProxyLogon</u></a> and <a href="https://www.mandiant.com/resources/pst-want-shell-proxyshell-exploiting-microsoft-exchange-servers" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>ProxyShell</u></a> vulnerabilities to gain initial access.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">c:\windows\system32\inetsrv\w3wp.exe, -ap, MSExchangeOWAAppPool, -v, v4.0, -c, C:\Program Files\Microsoft\Exchange Server\V15\bin\GenericAppPoolConfigWithGCServerEnabledFalse.config, -a, \\.\pipe\[redacted], -h, C:\inetpub\temp\apppools\MSExchangeOWAAppPool\MSExchangeOWAAppPool.config, -w, (empty), -m, 0</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div></pre>
<p class="mb-6">In each case REF9019 activity was traced back to Windows servers running Microsoft’s Exchange Server. Although we do not have information on the patch levels of those machines at the time of the execution or the exact vulnerabilities exploited, there is corroborating evidence regarding the exploitation of publicly accessible Exchange servers at this time generally, as well as specific reporting tied to the CUBA threat actor exploiting them.</p>
<p class="mb-6">This information combined with the lack of activity preceding this event, as well as the order of tactics after, indicates that in both cases exploitation of publicly accessible Exchange servers initiated the compromise.</p>
<blockquote class="my-7 border-l-4 border-default pl-4 text-lg text-subtler italic">
<p class="mb-6">While analyzing certain alerts throughout these events, we used data present in the process.Ext.memory_region.bytes_compressed field, and the technique we described in our <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/collecting-cobalt-strike-beacons-with-the-elastic-stack" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Cobalt Strike series</a>, to extract the memory-resident binaries and shellcode.</p>
</blockquote>
<h2 id="establish-foothold" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Establish Foothold</h2>
<h3 id="afk.ttf" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">afk.ttf</h3>
<p class="mb-6">This exploitation attempt preceded one primary infection by about 6 weeks. It appears a tactics shift occurred in the intervening period.</p>
<p class="mb-6">The file afk.ttf has been identified as a variant of “ZenPak” by some vendors on VirusTotal. ZenPak is categorized as a generic Trojan which has been associated with the Bazar malware family. The BazarBackdoor has a long history and was recently sighted in ransomware-as-a-service campaigns.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Initially, afk.ttf was identified through a malicious_file alert when it was created by the IIS worker process (w3wp.exe) handling the Exchange Service.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3cd9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blta655e83fb25139f5/6a7c7b2405b7b50b47185992/image14-cuba-ransomware-elastic.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">The afk.ttf file is a 64-bit Windows DLL that has a single export, bkfkals. Next, afk.ttf is loaded by rundll32.exe (spawned by w3wp.exe) which unpacks shellcode in memory and executes it. The unpacked shellcode is a Meterpreter payload from the offensive security framework, <a href="https://github.com/rapid7/metasploit-framework" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Metasploit</a>.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3ce9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt018d6ac3a22625d8/6a7c7b27de23155f28fd1d18/image16-cuba-ransomware-elastic.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Following this, afk.ttf uses an injection technique that allows the injected code to run before the entry point of the main thread of the process. This is known as <a href="https://www.cyberbit.com/endpoint-security/new-early-bird-code-injection-technique-discovered/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Early Bird injection</a> and is used in this situation to inject the shellcode in a suspended process for nslookup 8.8.8.8. Once the shellcode was deobfuscated for execution, the Elastic Agent identified and prevented the Metasploit payload.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3cf9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc76a23484d63c55d/6a7c7b2a73d9bd5dc7297b3e/image10-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Using the process.Ext.memory_region.bytes_compressed field we were able to recover the memory snapshot from these two alerts and verified that the shellcode was Meterpreter, which is part of the Metasploit framework. Additionally, we were able to extract the C2 IP (159.203.70[.]39) and URI (/Time/cb6zubbpio...truncated...).</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3cg9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltdde881c056cb194f/6a7c7b2d77b034573a3f94e9/image27-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Ultimately this foothold was either never established, or abandoned because there is no further activity from this endpoint until it is re-exploited about 6 weeks later.</p>
<h3 id="add2.exe" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">add2.exe</h3>
<p class="mb-6">The primary execution chain of both infections started with a malicious_file alert that fired upon the creation and execution of add2.exe by the IIS worker process handling the Exchange service. This was the same technique observed previously with the afk.ttf attempt. Interestingly, these executions happened within about 15 minutes of each other on victims in different countries and different industry verticals.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3ci9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltbe606c2fb5d1039c/6a7c7b302f00b23608ef8d72/image24-cuba-ransomware-elastic.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">The Elastic Malware Analysis and Reverse Engineering (MARE) team was able to <a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/728994be6b928de3d1c7b49ca1c79db8656c1cf4b95a1e508a6be48c6ab407da/detection" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>find this file in VirusTotal</u></a> and pull it down for binary analysis.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">BOOL sub_4013B0()

<p class="mb-6">MARE determined that this executable performs several functions:</p>
<p class="mb-6">Enumerates local administrator and RDP groups.</p>

<p class="mb-6">In total, add2.exe establishes local persistence via a hidden user and opening of a remote access service. This enables the REF9019 actor to connect back to this machine in case of discovery, patching of the vulnerability, or an incomplete eviction.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Additionally, VirusTotal indicated on the <a href="https://www.virustotal.com/graph/728994be6b928de3d1c7b49ca1c79db8656c1cf4b95a1e508a6be48c6ab407da" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">graph page</a> that this file has been hosted at <code class="border border-default bg-gray-50 px-2 font-mono text-sm">http://208.76.253[.]84</code>.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Of particular note, within the strings of add2.exe, we identified a unique program database file (PDB) named AddUser.pdb. PDB files are used to map elements of source code to the compiled program.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3cqpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt7879f0d4300a75aa/6a7c7b33227b1c403a5925cb/image8-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Searching in VirusTotal for the HEX value of F:\Source\WorkNew17\ (​​content:{463a5c536f757263655c576f726b4e65773137}), we identified another file named ad.exe which shared the same folder structure, and included another PDB file, CmdDLL.pdb.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3crpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2ff5d87878d49fab/6a7c7b36bdcff028fac3cfe9/image9-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">VirusTotal shows on the <a href="https://www.virustotal.com/graph/http%253A%252F%252F108.170.31.115%252Fadd.dll" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">graph page</a> that this file has been hosted at `<a href="http://108.170.31%5B.%5D115/add.dll%60%60" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">http://108.170.31[.]115/add.dll``</a>. While we did not observe add.dll, we believe they are related and have included the name, hash, and IP in our Observables table as the IP address (108.170.31[.]115) was also <a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/ip-address/108.170.31.115/relations" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">reported</a> distributing ra.exe (see the NetSupport section below).</p>
<p class="mb-6">Using this same search criteria, we were able to locate <a href="https://any.run/report/bd270853db17f94c2b8e4bd9fa089756a147ed45cbc44d6c2b0c78f361978906/e1579345-7571-4dcb-af4c-e74a4a81d804" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">three</a> <a href="https://any.run/report/2213db3f856cbed85a52cfa0275fa6eaba8e852a7e78449ab469d85d1945dc80/ed6077c3-6e2a-4f74-a761-cc0a354cc159" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">other</a> <a href="https://www.joesandbox.com/analysis/417560/1/html" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">files</a> with the same PDB debugging artifacts.<a href="https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.systembc" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>SystemBC</u></a> is a socks5 backdoor with the ability to communicate over TOR.</p>
<h2 id="remote-access-tools" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Remote Access Tools</h2>
<p class="mb-6">After establishing a beachhead, REF9019 dropped tooling to manage the post-exploitation phase of the attacks. Notably all tools were not present in each attack. It’s unclear if the decision to use one tool over another was merely driven by preference of individual operators, or if there was an operational factor that contributed to the decision.</p>
<h3 id="systembc" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">SystemBC</h3>
<p class="mb-6"><a href="https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.systembc" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>SystemBC</u></a> is a socks5 backdoor with the ability to communicate over TOR.</p>
<p class="mb-6">It was identified via malware_signature alerts that ran after SystemBC was injected into a svchost.exe process.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3cvpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc76a23484d63c55d/6a7c7b2a73d9bd5dc7297b3e/image10-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Post processing of the compressed_bytes of the shellcode_thread alert exposed network indicators our sample utilized, including its command and control server (104.217.8[.]100:5050).</p>
<blockquote class="my-7 border-l-4 border-default pl-4 text-lg text-subtler italic">
<p class="mb-6">Check out AhnLab’s ASEC blog for <a href="https://asec.ahnlab.com/en/33600/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">detailed coverage of SystemBC’s features</a>.</p>
</blockquote>
<p class="mb-6">Let’s look at the data for the SystemBC binary that was collected from the process.Ext.memory_region.bytes_compressed field.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3d1pltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc1617940615ddcf7/6a7c7b39bd2198126d7521d3/image15-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">If we run this through the strings command, it becomes a bit more readable. As mentioned above, the work done by the team at ASEC does a tremendous job of describing the SystemBC remote access tool, so we’ll focus on the atomic indicators that we observed.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">…truncated…

<p class="mb-6">The values of HOST1 and HOST2 are <a href="https://bazaar.abuse.ch/sample/6b36e8569a8b6150d760c3cfa1f23ff6072d2ab3bedd3633dc269ea6fd5fff9e/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>well-documented</u></a><a href="https://otx.alienvault.com/indicator/file/b9446c5f3fb7dd19e9f495d88431df2f0899ecb2" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u> infrastructure</u></a> for the SystemBC tool. The list of 10 IP addresses is Tor <a href="https://metrics.torproject.org/glossary.html#directory-authority" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>directory authorities</u></a>. One IP address is selected from the list to get the <a href="https://metrics.torproject.org/glossary.html#consensus" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>consensus data</u></a> for the Tor network. Then it will start Tor communications based on the settings it received (as previously reported by ASEC).</p>
<p class="mb-6">While we were not able to identify if Tor traffic was executed, this could have been a clandestine way to exfiltrate sensitive data.</p>
<h3 id="gotoassist" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">GoToAssist</h3>
<p class="mb-6"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RescueAssist" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>GoToAssist</u></a> is a remote desktop support application with some legitimate usage, but also known for its use in tech support scams.In this incident, it was used to download a malicious DLL to the newly created user’s downloads directory (C:\Users\Mysql\Downloads\94-79.dll). We were unable to collect this file and have not observed it later in the incident, however previous reporting has indicated use in CUBA campaigns of DLLs with similar naming conventions.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3d59ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb9b1e0c88cda1c2d/6a7c7b3c51156a57cc2bc802/image21-cuba-ransomware-elastic.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h3 id="netsupport" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">NetSupport</h3>
<p class="mb-6">NetSupport Manager is another client-server remote desktop management application. In this incident, NetSupport was named ra.exe and was written and executed from the C:\programdata\ directory by the previously exploited IIS worker process (w3wp.exe). ra.exe has been distributed by a previously identified IP address (see add2.exe section above).</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3d6pltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt07df3b880ffb8234/6a7c7b3fe3a2198bb6999662/image4-cuba-ransomware-elastic.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Our sample is the <a href="https://malpedia.caad.fkie.fraunhofer.de/details/win.netsupportmanager_rat" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>NetSupportManager RAT</u></a> as indicated on <a href="https://www.virustotal.com/gui/file/5669f6a48dac80717fa5770fa3be6c18022a7633b996ccf0df6b468994085378" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>VirusTotal</u></a> and corroborates <a href="https://www.mandiant.com/resources/unc2596-cuba-ransomware" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>prior reporting</u></a> of its usage with the CUBA Ransomware group.When analyzing the process data that we extracted from memory we can see that</p>
<h3 id="cobalt-strike" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Cobalt Strike</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Cobalt Strike was used in these intrusions, we confirmed this while reviewing the value of the <a href="https://docs.elastic.co/en/integrations/endpoint" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>Target.process.thread.Ext.start_address_bytes</u></a> (a few (typically 32) raw opcode bytes at the thread start address, hex-encoded). Upon doing this, we observed bytes commonly observed in Cobalt Strike payloads.</p>
<p class="mb-6">When analyzing the process data that we extracted from memory we can see that dhl.jpg (from mvnetworking[.]com) and temp.png (from bluetechsupply[.]com) are being used for command and control. This is corroborated by <a href="https://twitter.com/drb_ra/status/1482117406122201095" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>previous </u></a><a href="https://www.darktrace.com/en/inside-the-soc/how-antigena-intercepted-and-delayed-a-cobalt-strike-intrusion/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>research</u></a>.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3d99ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt12b48089b886f94e/6a7c7b42b4377093094d0f50/image6-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3d9pltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blted3e4c9d1e361c79/6a7c7b45ead8ecfbe5ba4845/image3-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Looking at the domains in Shodan ([<a href="https://www.shodan.io/search?query=mvnetworking.com" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>1</u></a>][<a href="https://www.shodan.io/search?query=bluetechsupply.com" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>2</u></a>]), we can see that they are both categorized as Cobalt Strike beacon C2 infrastructure.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dapltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt755b126d3a3edbc0/6a7c7b4796b5a6bbf08754c2/image25-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3db9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt814dbb5c31b6a823/6a7c7b4a9f5251961d663f80/image13-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">Both sites are hosted by a cloud provider, Hivelocity, Inc. We have requested the domains be taken down.</p>
<h3 id="bughatch" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">BUGHATCH</h3>
<p class="mb-6">BUGHATCH is the name given to a Cuba Ransomware associated downloader by Mandiant in their blog on <a href="https://www.mandiant.com/resources/unc2596-cuba-ransomware" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>UNC2596</u></a>. We detail the observed execution chain and indicators below.</p>
<p class="mb-6">BUGHATCH was launched via PowerShell script stagers in both cases. One execution was following the dropping of a malicious DLL to the Mysql user’s downloads folder (C:\Users\Mysql\Downloads\14931s.dll). Download URI for the next stage was found in the Target.process.Ext.memory_region.strings (<code class="border border-default bg-gray-50 px-2 font-mono text-sm">http://64.235.39[.]82/Agent32.bin</code>).</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3ddpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt040235eb10fe407b/6a7c7b4c51156aadcb2bc806/image26-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">In the above example, we observed agsyst82.ps1 downloading Agent32.bin from 64.235.39[.]82, but were unable to collect the PowerShell script. However, while performing open-source research, we identified a PowerShell script on ANY.RUN that performed network connections to the same IP and URL (<code class="border border-default bg-gray-50 px-2 font-mono text-sm">http://64.235.39[.]82/Agent32.bin</code>). The script is named komar.ps1 in ANY.RUN’s analysis. We are associating these two PowerShell scripts and network activity together.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3depltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt05b61e22e437458c/6a7c7b4f33fa8a24741fc865/image2-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">The other PowerShell script was called by a malicious file, cps.exe. This PowerShell script is called komar2.ps1 and downloads Agent32.bin from 38.108.119[.]121.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dfpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltbaed5bfdd85fba3e/6a7c7b52bdcff01029c3cfed/image28-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">komar2.ps1 next attempts to inject itself into svchost.exe from C:\Windows\Sysnative\svchost.exe.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dgpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt611e5a3bfc9b7fca/6a7c7b54227b1c79295925d1/image17-cuba-ransomware-elastic.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<blockquote class="my-7 border-l-4 border-default pl-4 text-lg text-subtler italic">
<p class="mb-6">For context, the C:\Windows\Sysnative path is a legitimate Windows directory and used to allow 32-bit applications to access the System32 folder on a 64-bit version of Windows. This path has also been observed as a <a href="https://thedfirreport.com/2021/08/29/cobalt-strike-a-defenders-guide/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">SpawnTo parameter</a> in Cobalt Strike process injection configurations.</p>
</blockquote>
<p class="mb-6">This new injected process again executes komar2.ps1 and includes a new PDB entry of F:\Source\Mosquito\Agent\x64\Release\Agent.pdb. As we discussed above, “komar” means “mosquito” in Polish and is a good indicator as a way to identify other related entities; we see “Mosquito” in the path of the PDB. While a weak association by itself, the PDB in this sample is located in F:\Source, which is the same location that we’d observed with F:\Source\WorkNew## above for add2.exe. By themselves, they are not a solid reference point between the two samples, but when compared together, they can be categorized as “interesting”.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Based on analysis of the Agent32.bin file, we believe that this is the BUGHATCH malware. BUGHATCH has been observed being used as a downloader in CUBA ransomware incidents. This aligns to how we observed Agent32.bin. BUGHATCH has been <a href="https://www.mandiant.com/resources/unc2596-cuba-ransomware" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>covered in the UNC2596 blog</u></a> by the team at Mandiant.</p>
<h2 id="credential-harvesting,-internal-reconnaissance,-and-lateral-movement" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Credential Harvesting, Internal Reconnaissance, and Lateral Movement</h2>
<p class="mb-6">Credential harvesting was observed through process injection into the GoToAssistUnattendedUi.exe binaries. These appear to be the legitimate files for the Go To Assist suite. The credential harvesting was accomplished by using Meterpreter and Mimikatz.</p>
<h3 id="meterpreter" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Meterpreter</h3>
<p class="mb-6">As we observed in the initial infection several months prior, Meterpreter was observed being used to collect the SAM database using the <a href="https://www.offensive-security.com/metasploit-unleashed/meterpreter-basics/#:~:text=SYSTEM%0Ameterpreter%20%3E-,hashdump,-The%20hashdump%20post" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>hashdump module</u></a>. As previously, this was observed in the Target.process.Ext.memory_region.strings fields.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dkpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltc0d98ed7858048f3/6a7c7b57de2315d2c3fd1d1c/image18-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h3 id="mimikatz" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Mimikatz</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Similarly to the Meterpreter tool markings, we also observed <a href="https://attack.mitre.org/software/S0002/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>Mimikatz</u></a>. Mimikatz is an offensive security tool used to collect and inject passwords from compromised systems. It uses the <a href="https://adsecurity.org/?page_id=1821#SEKURLSALogonPasswords" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>SEKURLSA::LogonPasswords</u></a> module to list all available provider credentials, and this was observed in the Target.process.Ext.memory_region.strings fields.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dm9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6f5f4ec673bcd741/6a7c7b5a33fa8a25d41fc86b/image22-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h3 id="zerologon-exploit" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Zerologon Exploit</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Next the threat actors attempted to use a file called zero.exe, which is used to exploit the <a href="https://msrc.microsoft.com/update-guide/vulnerability/CVE-2020-1472" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>Zerologon vulnerability</u></a> to escalate privileges. This file is referenced in <a href="https://thedfirreport.com/2021/11/01/from-zero-to-domain-admin/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>previous reporting</u></a> and is executed on a vulnerable domain controller to dump the NTLM hash for the Administrator. This is a common tactic for lateral movement and to deploy additional implants into the environment, such as Cobalt Strike.</p>
<h3 id="psexec" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">PsExec</h3>
<p class="mb-6"><a href="https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/sysinternals/downloads/psexec" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>PsExec</u></a> is a legitimate utility, part of the SysInternals suite of tools, used to interactively launch processes on remote systems. PsExec is a common tool for remote administration, both benign and malicious.</p>
<p class="mb-6">While we cannot validate how specifically PsExec was used because there was not an SMB parser on the infected hosts, we can see that PsExec was used to move files between the infected hosts. We cannot confirm that this was not normal administration by the local IT staff, but the only activity observed was between infected hosts and was within the time window of other confirmed malicious activity.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dp9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb2c98e2b6665b9e5/6a7c7b5c80ee38b4fe60d0b4/image5-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h3 id="using-lolbas" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Using LOLBAS</h3>
<p class="mb-6"><a href="https://lolbas-project.github.io/#" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>Living off the land binaries, scripts, and libraries (LOLBAS)</u></a> is a commonly leveraged method to use native and benign tools for malicious purposes. This reduces attacker tools that need to be moved into the environment as well as to appear more like legitimate processes running in a targeted environment.</p>
<p class="mb-6">In one intrusion we observed PsExec being used to remotely copy files (see the PsExec section), however in another environment, we observed similar activity to move files using cmd.exe to move files from one host to another. We were unable to collect the files that were being moved for analysis, but they were a DLL and a Batch file named d478.dll and d478.bat, and the atomic indicators are stored in the Observations table.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dr9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blta5b4f92b04151753/6a7c7b5ffc63ab684c646e62/image11-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h2 id="data-exfiltration" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Data Exfiltration</h2>
<p class="mb-6">The CUBA group belongs to a variant of ransomware operators in that they use extortion as a mechanism to coerce payments from their victims.</p>
<p class="mb-6">In these situations, once initial access and a foothold is achieved, threat actors will identify potentially sensitive data and exfiltrate it off of the environment to use for threats of “name and shame”.</p>
<p class="mb-6">The CUBA group runs a website on the dark web where they release data from victims that do not pay. CUBA releases some data for free, and for others that are more lucrative, have a payment option.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dtpltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb01dd2817d76dec8/6a7c7b62e02facd4485d0401/image7-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3du9ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltef46cf1e40674165/6a7c7b65de2315b33ffd1d20/image20-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3dupltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt07df8b1fa746076a/6a7c7b68227b1c59ec5925d5/image12-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<p class="mb-6">There are multiple ways that the victim data could have been exfiltrated for extortion, the presence of BUGHATCH, Meterpreter, and Cobalt Strike all have data movement capabilities.</p>
<h2 id="defense-evasion-and-actions-on-the-objective" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Defense Evasion and Actions on the Objective</h2>
<h3 id="defendercontrol.exe" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">DefenderControl.exe</h3>
<p class="mb-6">To prevent the detection of their malware, the threat actors used <a href="https://www.sordum.org/9480/defender-control-v2-1/" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>Defender Control</u></a> as a way to disable Microsoft Defender, the native antivirus built into all Windows systems since Vista.</p>
<p class="mb-6">To ensure that Defender Control continued to run, the threat actor used svchost.exe to create a scheduled task.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3e1pltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt2d2693a42ad6ad50/6a7c7b6b4c4bfb8725cc77ef/image1-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h3 id="cuba-ransomware" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">CUBA Ransomware</h3>
<p class="mb-6">We detail the observed execution chain and indicators above, but please see Elastic MARE’s detailed reverse engineering of this sample <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/cuba-ransomware-malware-analysis" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">here</a>.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3e39ltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltaf89000529a0ec44/6a7c7b6d2f00b208e0ef8d76/image223-cuba-ransomware-elastic.jpg" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h2 id="diamond-model" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Diamond Model</h2>
<p class="mb-6">Elastic Security utilizes the <a href="https://www.activeresponse.org/wp-content/uploads/2013/07/diamond.pdf" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3"><u>Diamond Model</u></a> to describe high-level relationships between the adversaries, capabilities, infrastructure, and victims of intrusions. While the Diamond Model is most commonly used with single intrusions, and leveraging Activity Threading (section 8) as a way to create relationships between incidents, an adversary-centered (section 7.1.4) approach allows for a, although cluttered, single diamond.</p>
<p class="mb-6"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3e4pltnl6:"><img alt="" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltb384137acc45f696/6a7c7b70e88c65560f00575d/image224-cuba-ransomware-elastic.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button></p>
<h2 id="observed-adversary-tactics-and-techniques" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Observed Adversary Tactics and Techniques</h2>
<h3 id="tactics" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Tactics</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Using the MITRE ATT&amp;CK® framework, tactics represent the why of a technique or sub technique. It is the adversary’s tactical goal: the reason for performing an action.</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Initial access</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Persistence</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Privilege escalation</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Defense evasion</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Credential access</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Discovery</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Lateral movement</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Command &amp; Control</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Exfiltration</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Impact</li>
</ul>
<p class="mb-6">It should be noted that we did not observe the Collection tactic, but based on the evidence of Exfiltration and Impact, this would have been completed.</p>
<h3 id="techniques-/-sub-techniques" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Techniques / Sub Techniques</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Techniques and Sub techniques represent how an adversary achieves a tactical goal by performing an action.</p>
<p class="mb-6">As noted throughout this research, this covered multiple victims over a large period of time. The CUBA intrusion set has been reported using different techniques and sub techniques, but these are our specific observations.</p>
<p class="mb-6">Observed techniques/sub techniques.</p>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Exploit Public-Facing Application</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Command and Scripting Interpreter - PowerShell, Windows Command Shell</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Scheduled Task/Job - Scheduled Task</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Boot or Logon Autostart Execution - Registry Run Keys/Startup Folder</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Create Account - Local Account</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">OS Credential Dumping - LSA Secrets</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Data Encrypted for Impact</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Hide Artifact - Hidden Window</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Masquerading - Match Legitimate Name or Location</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Obfuscated Files or Information</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">Reflective Code Loading</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="detection" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Detection</h2>
<h3 id="yara" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">YARA</h3>
<p class="mb-6">Elastic Security has created YARA rules to identify this BUGHATCH and CUBA ransomware activity.</p>
<pre><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">rule Windows_Trojan_Bughatch {
    meta:
        author = &quot;Elastic Security&quot;
        creation_date = &quot;2022-05-09&quot;
        last_modified = &quot;2022-05-09&quot;
        os = &quot;Windows&quot;
        arch = &quot;x86&quot;
        category_type = &quot;Trojan&quot;
        family = &quot;Bughatch&quot;
        threat_name = &quot;Windows.Trojan.Bughatch&quot;
        reference_sample = &quot;b495456a2239f3ba48e43ef295d6c00066473d6a7991051e1705a48746e8051f&quot;
    strings:
        $a1 = { 8B 45 ?? 33 D2 B9 A7 00 00 00 F7 F1 85 D2 75 ?? B8 01 00 00 00 EB 33 C0 }
        $a2 = { 8B 45 ?? 0F B7 48 04 81 F9 64 86 00 00 75 3B 8B 55 ?? 0F B7 42 16 25 00 20 00 00 ?? ?? B8 06 00 00 00 EB ?? }
        $b1 = { 69 4D 10 FD 43 03 00 81 C1 C3 9E 26 00 89 4D 10 8B 55 FC 8B 45 F8 0F B7 0C 50 8B 55 10 C1 EA 10 81 E2 FF FF 00 00 33 CA 8B 45 FC 8B 55 F8 66 89 0C 42 }
        $c1 = &quot;-windowstyle hidden -executionpolicy bypass -file&quot;
        $c2 = &quot;C:\\Windows\\SysWOW64\\WindowsPowerShell\\v1.0\\PowerShell.exe&quot;
        $c3 = &quot;ReflectiveLoader&quot;
        $c4 = &quot;\\Sysnative\\&quot;
        $c5 = &quot;TEMP%u.CMD&quot;
        $c6 = &quot;TEMP%u.PS1&quot;
        $c7 = &quot;\\TEMP%d.%s&quot;
        $c8 = &quot;NtSetContextThread&quot;
        $c9 = &quot;NtResumeThread&quot;
    condition:
        ($a1 or $a2 or $b1) or 6 of ($c*)
}

rule Windows_Ransomware_Cuba {
    meta:
        os = &quot;Windows&quot;
        arch = &quot;x86&quot;
        category_type = &quot;Ransomware&quot;
        family = &quot;Cuba&quot;
        threat_name = &quot;Windows.Ransomware.Cuba&quot;
        Reference_sample =
&quot;33352a38454cfc247bc7465bf177f5f97d7fd0bd220103d4422c8ec45b4d3d0e&quot;

    strings:
       $a1 = { 45 EC 8B F9 8B 45 14 89 45 F0 8D 45 E4 50 8D 45 F8 66 0F 13 }
       $a2 = { 8B 06 81 38 46 49 44 45 75 ?? 81 78 04 4C 2E 43 41 74 }
      $b1 = &quot;We also inform that your databases, ftp server and file server were downloaded by us to our     servers.&quot; ascii fullword
      $b2 = &quot;Good day. All your files are encrypted. For decryption contact us.&quot; ascii fullword
       $b3 = &quot;.cuba&quot; wide fullword

    condition:
        any of ($a*) or all of ($b*)
}</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div></pre>
<h2 id="defensive-recommendations" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Defensive Recommendations</h2>
<ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc">
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">
<p class="mb-6">Enable Elastic Security Memory and Ransomware protections</p>
</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">
<p class="mb-6">Review and ensure that you have deployed the latest Microsoft Security Updates</p>
</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">
<p class="mb-6">Maintain backups of your critical systems to aid in quick recovery</p>
</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">
<p class="mb-6">Attack surface reduction</p>
</li>
<li class="text-subtler mb-4">
<p class="mb-6">Network segmentation</p>
</li>
</ul>
<h2 id="observations" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7">Observations</h2>
<p class="mb-6">Atomic indicators observed in our investigation.</p>
